).

Composition of wiring diagrams is visually straightforward. For example, the picture below shows four wiring diagrams: two "interior" wiring diagrams
\varphi_{1}\colon X_{11},X_{12},X_{13}\to Y_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}\colon X_{21},X_{22}\to Y_{2}
, an "exterior" wiring diagram
\psi\colon Y_{1},Y_{2}\to Z
(shown again on the right):

\scriptstyle X_{11}
\scriptstyle X_{12}
\scriptstyle X_{13}

\scriptstyle X_{21}
\scriptstyle X_{22}

\scriptstyle Y_{1}
\scriptstyle Y_{2}
\scriptstyle Z

Y_{2}
Y_{1}
Z

From
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
, and
\psi
, we can erase the dashed boxes and derive a five-box wiring diagram
X_{11},X_{12},X_{13},X_{21},X_{22}\to Z
. We call it their
composition
and denote it
\omega=\psi\circ(\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2})
. This corresponds to the composition of
X\xrightarrow{\varphi}Y\xrightarrow{\psi}Z
in a symmetric monoidal category
{W}
as described in Definition
3.10
, where
X=X_{11}+X_{12}+X_{13}+X_{12}+X_{22}
and
Y=Y_{1}+Y_{2}
.

Definition 3.10
.

Let
{C}
be a finite product category. Given wiring diagrams
\varphi\colon X\to Y
and
\psi\colon Y\to Z
, we define their
composition
, denoted
\psi\circ\varphi\colon X\to Z
, by the following compositions in
\mathbf{TFS}_{{C}}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\textnormal{in}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\textnormal{in}}+X^{\textnormal{out}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\textnormal{in}}+X^{\textnormal{out}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\textnormal{in}}+Y^{\textnormal{out}}+X^{\textnormal{out}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\textnormal{in}}+X^{\textnormal{out}}+X^{\textnormal{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{in}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\textnormal{in}}+X^{\textnormal{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\textnormal{in}}+\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}+X^{\textnormal{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\textnormal{in}}+\nabla_{X^{\textnormal{out}}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{\textnormal{out}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\textnormal{out}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\textnormal{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{\textnormal{out}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}

It is straightforward to show that this composition formula is associative and unital. Thus we have defined
the category of
{C}
-boxes and wiring diagrams
, which we denote
{W}_{{C}}
. This category has a symmetric monoidal structure
(\square,\boxplus)
, where
\square
is the closed box and
\boxplus
is given by sums of boxes and wiring diagrams, as in Definition
3.1
.

Remark 3.11
.

A wiring diagram
\varphi\colon X\to Y
, includes two typed functions
\varphi^{\textnormal{in}},\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}
, which have as dependent product the functions
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}},\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}
(see Definition
3.3
) as shown below:

\displaystyle\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}

\displaystyle\colon X^{\textnormal{in}}\to Y^{\textnormal{in}}+X^{\textnormal{out}}

\displaystyle\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}

\displaystyle\colon Y^{\textnormal{out}}\to X^{\textnormal{out}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}

\displaystyle\colon\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\to\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}

\displaystyle\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}

\displaystyle\colon\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\to\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}}

The proof of following lemma is a straightforward rewriting of Definition
3.10
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Suppose given wiring diagrams
\varphi\colon X\to Y
and
\psi\colon Y\to Z
. Then the dependent products
\widehat{(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{in}}}\colon\widehat{Z^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\to\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
\widehat{(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{out}}}\colon\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\to\widehat{Z^{\textnormal{out}}}
are given by the formulas

\displaystyle\widehat{(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{in}}}(z,x)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\Big(\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{in}}}\big(z,\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}(x)\big),x\Big)

\displaystyle\widehat{(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{out}}}(x)

\displaystyle=\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{out}}}\Big(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}(x)\Big)

Chapter 4
Five formal interpretations of wiring diagrams

In this final section, we give precise formulas for putting together subsystems according to an arbitrary wiring diagram, to form a larger system. These systems may be dynamical systems of various kinds (discrete, measurable, linear, continuous) or they may be matrices; we call these our five
interpretations
of wiring diagrams. Two of them, namely linear and continuous systems, are taken from
[
VSL15
]
. Another, namely discrete systems, is loosely adapted from
[
SR13
]
. Technically, each interpretation is a lax
\mathbf{Set}
-valued functor on
{W}
, the symmetric monoidal category of wiring diagrams as defined in Section
3
. Experts may note that algebras on the operad of wiring diagrams appear related to traced monoidal categories, and indeed they are; see
[
JSV96
]
and
[
SSR15
]
.

We spell out how each interpretation works in several steps. In Section
13
, we remind the reader what sort of thing is allowed to fill, or
inhabit
, a given box shape, in each of our five interpretations. In Section
14
, we explain what happens when boxes are put into parallel. In fact, whenever a wiring diagram includes several boxes, the default technique is that of Example
3.9
: First we put them in parallel to form one box, and then we use a wiring diagram that has only that one inner box (see Definition
3.8
). Thus we complete our description of our five interpretations in Section
15
by saying what happens on wiring diagrams (with one inner box).
3
3

3

Note that in practice, this default ”tensor then wire” technique is almost never the most efficient. For example, multiplying two matrices can be obtained by tensoring them and tracing the result, but this requires an order of magnitude more operations than the usual matrix product formula. In the extended example, Section
17
, we show an alternative technique. The proofs in the present section imply that all techniques will give the same final answer.

In Section
16
we give some compositional maps between interpretations. Most of these have been briefly discussed earlier in the paper, but we make formal theorems here. We conclude in Section
17
with an extended example.

13
Inhabitants of a box

Definitions
4.1
,
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
, and
4.5
say precisely the set of inhabitants that are allowed to fill each box
X\in{W}
(e.g., (
10
)), according to our five interpretations: discrete systems, measurable systems, linear systems, continuous systems, and matrices. In this section, we are simply gathering together Definitions from Section
2
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
{C}=\mathbf{Set}
, and let
X=(X^{\textnormal{in}},X^{\textnormal{out}})\in{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}
be a
\mathbf{Set}
-box. Define
\mathsf{DS}(X)\coloneqq\mathsf{DS}(\widehat{X})
to be the set of
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}},\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-discrete systems, as in Definition
2.1
. That is,

\mathsf{DS}(X)\coloneqq\left\{(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\;\;\middle|\;\;S\in\mathbf{Set},\quad f^{\textnormal{rdt}}\in\mathbf{Set}\left(S,\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\right),\quad f^{\textnormal{upd}}\in\mathbf{Set}\left(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times S,S\right)\right\}

Definition 4.2
.

Let
{C}=\mathbf{CSMeas}
, and let
X=(X^{\textnormal{in}},X^{\textnormal{out}})\in{W}_{\mathbf{CSMeas}}
be a
\mathbf{CSMeas}
-box. Define
\mathsf{MS}(X)\coloneqq\mathsf{MS}(\widehat{X})
to be the set of
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}},\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-measurable systems, as in Definition
2.13
. That is,

\mathsf{DS}(X)\coloneqq\left\{(S,\mu,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\;\;\middle|\;\;\parbox{224.03743pt}{$S\in\mathbf{CSMeas},\quad\mu\textnormal{ is a measure on }S,\\
f^{\textnormal{rdt}}\in\mathbf{Set}\left(S,\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\right),\quad f^{\textnormal{upd}}\in\mathbf{Set}\left(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times S,S\right)$}\right\}

Definition 4.3
.

Let
{C}=\mathbf{Lin}
, and let
X=(X^{\textnormal{in}},X^{\textnormal{out}})\in{W}_{\mathbf{Lin}}
be a
\mathbf{Lin}
-box.
Define
\mathsf{LS}(X)\coloneqq\mathsf{LS}(\widehat{X})
to be the set of
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}},\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-linear systems, as in Definition
2.15
. That is,

\mathsf{LS}(X)\coloneqq\left\{(S,M^{\textnormal{in}},M^{\textnormal{mid}},M^{\textnormal{out}})\;\;\middle|\;\;S\in\mathbf{Lin},\;\begin{array}[]{ll}M^{\textnormal{mid}}\in\mathbf{Lin}(S,S)&M^{\textnormal{in}}\in\mathbf{Lin}\left(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}},S\right)\\
M^{\textnormal{out}}\in\mathbf{Lin}\left(S,\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\right)\end{array}\right\}

Definition 4.4
.

Let
{C}=\mathbf{Set}
, and let
X=(X^{\textnormal{in}},X^{\textnormal{out}})\in{W}_{\mathbf{Euc}}
be a
\mathbf{Euc}
-box.
Define
\mathsf{CS}(X)\coloneqq\mathsf{CS}(\widehat{X})
to be the set of
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}},\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-continuous systems, as in Definition
2.17
. That is,

\mathsf{CS}(X)\coloneqq\left\{(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{dyn}})\;\;\middle|\;\;S\in\mathbf{Euc},\quad f^{\textnormal{rdt}}\in\mathbf{Euc}\left(S,\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\right),\quad f^{\textnormal{upd}}\in\mathbf{Euc}_{/S}\left(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times S,TS\right)\right\}

Recall that if
S\in{C}
is an object, then
{C}_{/S}
denotes the slice category of
{C}
over
S
. We will not need this again; it was used in Definition
4.4
simply as shorthand for the diagram (
6
).

Definition 4.5
.

Let
{C}=\mathbf{Set}
, let
R
be a complete semiring, and let
X=(X^{\textnormal{in}},X^{\textnormal{out}})\in{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}
be a
\mathbf{Set}
-box. Define
\mathsf{Mat}_{R}(X)\coloneqq\mathsf{Mat}(\widehat{X})
to be the set of
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-matrices in
R
. This can be identified with the set of functions

\mathsf{Mat}(X)\cong\left\{M\colon\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\to R\right\}.

14
Parallelizing inhabitants

In this section we explain how parallel composition works for each of our five interpretations, discrete systems, measurable systems, linear systems, continuous systems, and matrices. One may refer to Example
3.2
and Definition
3.6
.

Definition 4.6
.

Suppose we are given discrete systems
F_{1}=(S_{1},f_{1}^{\textnormal{rdt}},f_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{DS}(X_{1})
and
F_{2}=(S_{2},f_{2}^{\textnormal{rdt}},f_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{DS}(X_{2})
. Their
parallel composition
, denoted by
F_{1}\boxtimes F_{2}=(T,g^{\textnormal{rdt}},g^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{DS}(X_{1}\boxplus X_{2})
is given as follows. Its state set is the product
T\coloneqq S_{1}\times S_{2}
in
\mathbf{Set}
, its readout function
g^{\textnormal{rdt}}=(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{rdt}}
is the product

(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{rdt}}\coloneqq f_{1}^{\textnormal{rdt}}\times f_{2}^{\textnormal{rdt}}\colon S_{1}\times S_{2}\to B_{1}\times B_{2},

and its update function
g^{\textnormal{upd}}=(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{upd}}
is the product
f_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}}\times f_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}}
as shown here:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\times A_{2}\times S_{1}\times S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}\times S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\times S_{1}\times A_{2}\times S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{1}\times S_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{upd}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}}\times f_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}}}

Remark 4.7
.

Definition
4.6
also makes sense when
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are assumed to be measurable systems, i.e., we can form a measurable system
F_{1}\boxtimes F_{2}
, called their
parallel composition
, in the identical way. In particular, the set
S_{1}\times S_{2}
is given the product measure
\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}
(see
[
Fre06
]
).

Definition 4.8
.

Suppose we are given linear systems
M_{1}=(S_{1},M_{1}^{\textnormal{in}},M_{1}^{\textnormal{mid}},M_{1}^{\textnormal{out}})
and
M_{2}=(S_{2},M_{2}^{\textnormal{in}},M_{2}^{\textnormal{mid}},M_{2}^{\textnormal{out}})
. Their
parallel composition
, denoted
M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
is simply given by direct sums of the respective linear maps:

(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})^{\textnormal{in}}\coloneqq M_{1}^{\textnormal{in}}\oplus M_{2}^{\textnormal{in}}\qquad(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})^{\textnormal{mid}}\coloneqq M_{1}^{\textnormal{mid}}\oplus M_{2}^{\textnormal{mid}}\qquad(M_{1}\oplus M_{2})^{\textnormal{out}}\coloneqq M_{1}^{\textnormal{out}}\oplus M_{2}^{\textnormal{out}}

Definition 4.9
.

Suppose we are given continuous systems
F_{1}=(S_{1},f_{1}^{\textnormal{rdt}},f_{1}^{\textnormal{dyn}})\in\mathsf{CS}(X_{1})
and
F_{2}=(S_{2},f_{2}^{\textnormal{rdt}},f_{2}^{\textnormal{dyn}})\in\mathsf{CS}(X_{2})
. Their
parallel composition
, denoted by
F_{1}\boxtimes F_{2}=(T,g^{\textnormal{rdt}},g^{\textnormal{dyn}})\in\mathsf{CS}(X_{1}\boxplus X_{2})
is given as follows. Its state set is the product
T\coloneqq S_{1}\times S_{2}
in
\mathbf{Euc}
, its readout function
g^{\textnormal{rdt}}=(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{rdt}}
is the product

(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{rdt}}\coloneqq f_{1}^{\textnormal{rdt}}\times f_{2}^{\textnormal{rdt}}\colon S_{1}\times S_{2}\to B_{1}\times B_{2},

and its update function
g^{\textnormal{upd}}=(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{upd}}
is, up to isomorphism, the product
f_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}}\times f_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}}
as shown here:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\times A_{2}\times S_{1}\times S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(S_{1}\times S_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\times S_{1}\times A_{2}\times S_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS_{1}\times TS_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{dyn}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{\textnormal{dyn}}\times f_{2}^{\textnormal{dyn}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Definition 4.10
.

Let
R
be a semiring. Suppose we are given
R
-matrices
M^{1}\in\mathsf{Mat}_{R}(X_{1})
and
M^{2}\in\mathsf{Mat}_{R}(X_{2})
. Their
parallel composition
, denoted by
M^{1}\otimes M^{2}\in\mathsf{Mat}_{R}(X_{1}\boxplus X_{2})
is given as the Kronecker product, given by component-wise product (in
R
):

(M^{1}\otimes M^{2})_{(i_{1},i_{2}),(j_{1},j_{2})}\coloneqq M^{1}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdot M^{2}_{i_{2},j_{2}}

(17)

15
Wiring together inhabitants

Any complex wiring diagram
\varphi\colon X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\to Y
, such as the one shown in (
1
), can be constructed by first putting the input boxes in parallel
X=X_{1}\boxplus\cdots\boxplus X_{n}
as in Definition
3.6
, and then using a wiring diagram
X\to Y
with a single inner box (see Example
3.9
). For each of our five interpretations (dynamical systems and matrices), the formula for putting together inhabitants of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
to form an inhabitant of
Y
is likewise done in these two steps. Parallelizing inhabitants was discussed in Section
14
and how a single inhabitant, wired into a larger box, produces an inhabitant of that larger box, is described in this section.

We not only give the formula, we also prove Theorems
4.12
,
4.14
,
4.18
,
4.20
, and
4.24
, which say that these formulas are coherent for each of our five interpretations. More formally, we prove they constitute lax monoidal functors.

Discrete systems

Definition 4.11
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to Y
be a wiring diagram in
{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}
, and suppose that
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{DS}(X)
is an
\widehat{X}
-discrete system. We define
the
\mathsf{DS}
-application of
\varphi
to
F
, denoted
\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)(F)\in\mathsf{DS}(Y)
, to be the
\widehat{Y}
-discrete system
\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)(F)=(T,g^{\textnormal{rdt}},g^{\textnormal{upd}})
where

T\coloneqq S,\quad g^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\coloneqq\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}\left(f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\right),\quad g^{\textnormal{upd}}(y,s)\coloneqq f^{\textnormal{upd}}\left(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\left(y,f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\right),s\right)

(18)

Theorem 4.12
.

The assignments
X\mapsto\mathsf{DS}(X)
and
\varphi\mapsto\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)
, with parallel composition as in Definition
4.6
constitute a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{DS}\colon{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Proof.

We need to check that for any
X\xrightarrow{\varphi}Y\xrightarrow{\psi}Z
and discrete system
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{DS}(X)
, the following equation holds:

\mathsf{DS}(\psi)\big(\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)(F)\big)=\mathsf{DS}(\psi\circ\varphi)(F).

For ease of notation, let
G=(T,g^{\textnormal{rdt}},g^{\textnormal{upd}})\coloneqq\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)(F)
, let
H_{1}=(U_{1},h_{1}^{\textnormal{rdt}},h_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}})\coloneqq\mathsf{DS}(\psi)(G)
, and let
H_{2}=(U_{2},h_{2}^{\textnormal{rdt}},h_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}})\coloneqq\mathsf{DS}(\psi\circ\varphi)(F)
. We want to show that
H_{1}=H_{2}
.

It is easy to see that they have the same state set,
U_{1}=U_{2}=S
, and the same readout function
h_{1}^{\textnormal{rdt}}=h_{2}^{\textnormal{rdt}}=\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{out}}}\circ\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}\circ f^{\textnormal{rdt}}
. We compute the update functions and see they are the same for any
z\in\widehat{Z^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
s\in S
:

\displaystyle h_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}}(z,s)

\displaystyle=g^{\textnormal{upd}}\Big(\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{in}}}(z,g^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\big),s\Big)

\displaystyle=f^{\textnormal{upd}}\bigg(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\Big(\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{in}}}\big(z,g^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\big),f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\Big),s\bigg)

\displaystyle=f^{\textnormal{upd}}\Bigg(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\bigg(\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{in}}}\Big(z,\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}\big(f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\big)\Big),f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\bigg),s\Bigg)

\displaystyle=f^{\textnormal{upd}}\Big(\widehat{(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{in}}}\big(z,f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\big),s\Big)

\displaystyle=h_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}}(z,s)

where the penultimate equality is an application of Lemma
3.12
, and the rest are merely untangling (
18
).

We also need to check that
\mathsf{DS}
is symmetric monoidal. This is straightforward; it follows from the fact that taking dependent products is itself symmetric monoidal, sending coproducts to products, as in Lemma
3.4
.
∎

Measurable systems

Definition 4.13
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to Y
be a wiring diagram in
{W}_{\mathbf{CSMeas}}
, and suppose that
F=(S,\mu,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{MS}(X)
is an
\widehat{X}
-measurable system. We define
the
\mathsf{MS}
-application of
\varphi
to
F
, denoted
\mathsf{MS}(\varphi)(F)\in\mathsf{DS}(Y)
, to be the
\widehat{Y}
-measurable system
\mathsf{MS}(\varphi)(F)=(T,\nu,g^{\textnormal{rdt}},g^{\textnormal{upd}})
where

(T,\nu)\coloneqq(S,\mu),\quad g^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\coloneqq\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}\left(f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\right),\quad g^{\textnormal{upd}}(y,s)\coloneqq f^{\textnormal{upd}}\left(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\left(y,f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\right),s\right)

(19)

Theorem 4.14
.

The assignments
X\mapsto\mathsf{MS}(X)
and
\varphi\mapsto\mathsf{MS}(\varphi)
, with parallel composition as in Remark
4.7
constitute a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{MS}\colon{W}_{\mathbf{CSMeas}}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Proof.

The underlying set functor
U\colon\mathbf{CSMeas}\to\mathbf{Set}
is faithful; that is, for any measurable functions
f,g\colon X\to Y
, if they agree on underlying sets,
U(f)=U(g)
then they are equal
f=g
. Suppose
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{MS}(X)
. Checking that the equation

\mathsf{MS}(\psi)\big(\mathsf{MS}(\varphi)(F)\big)=\mathsf{MS}(\psi\circ\varphi)(F)

holds is a matter of checking that both sides have the same state space (they do: both are
S
) and the same readout and update functions. Thus the functoriality follows from Theorem
4.12
by the faithfulness of
U
.

To see that
\mathsf{MS}
is monoidal, notice that

\mathsf{MS}(X)=\bigsqcup_{(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{DS}(X)}\{\mu\mid\mu\textnormal{ is a measure on }S\}.

Consider the functor
\mathbf{CSMeas}\to\mathbf{Set}
given by assigning the set of measures to a measurable space. It is monoidal, using the product measure construction
[
Fre06
]
, and the result follows.
∎

Linear systems

To define how wiring diagrams act on linear systems, we must first define the derivative of a wiring diagram.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to Y
be a wiring diagram in
{W}_{\mathbf{Lin}}
. We will define its
derivative
to be three linear functions
\Phi^{\textnormal{in}}\in\mathbf{Lin}(\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}},\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}})
,
\Phi^{\textnormal{mid}}\in\mathbf{Lin}(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}},\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}})
, and
\Phi^{\textnormal{out}}\in\mathbf{Lin}(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}},\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}})
by taking derivatives, as follows.

Recall that the dependent product
\widehat{\varphi}
consists of two parts
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\colon\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\times\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}\to\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}\colon\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\to\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}}
. We define the three matrices as the following derivatives:

\Phi^{\textnormal{in}}\coloneqq\partial_{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}},\qquad\Phi^{\textnormal{mid}}\coloneqq\partial_{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}},\qquad\Phi^{\textnormal{out}}\coloneqq\partial_{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}.

(20)

Note that because each of
\Phi^{\textnormal{in}}
,
\Phi^{\textnormal{mid}}
, and
\Phi^{\textnormal{out}}
is in fact a matrix of 1’s and 0’s because
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}
are dependent products (see Definition
3.3
), meaning that they are made up simply of projections and diagonal maps.

Lemma 4.16
.

Suppose that
\varphi\colon X\to Y
and
\psi\colon Y\to Z
are wiring diagrams with derivatives
\Phi=(\Phi^{\textnormal{in}},\Phi^{\textnormal{mid}},\Phi^{\textnormal{out}})
and
\Psi=(\Psi^{\textnormal{in}},\Psi^{\textnormal{mid}},\Psi^{\textnormal{out}})
as in Definition
4.15
. The the composite wiring diagram
\omega=\psi\circ\varphi\colon X\to Z
, as in Definition
3.10
has derivatives

\Omega^{\textnormal{in}}=\Phi^{\textnormal{in}}\Psi^{\textnormal{in}},\qquad\Omega^{\textnormal{mid}}=\Phi^{\textnormal{mid}}+\Phi^{\textnormal{in}}\Psi^{\textnormal{mid}}\Phi^{\textnormal{out}},\qquad\Omega^{\textnormal{out}}=\Psi^{\textnormal{out}}\Phi^{\textnormal{out}}.

Proof.

This is a chain rule computation, taking derivatives of the formulas in Lemma
3.12
.∎

Definition 4.17
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to Y
be a wiring diagram in
{W}_{\mathbf{Lin}}
and let
(\Phi^{\textnormal{in}},\Phi^{\textnormal{mid}},\Phi^{\textnormal{out}})
be its derivatives as in Definition
4.15
. Suppose that
M=(S,M^{\textnormal{in}},M^{\textnormal{mid}},M^{\textnormal{out}})\in\mathsf{LS}(X)
is an
\widehat{X}
-linear system. We define
the
\mathsf{LS}
-application of
\varphi
to
M
, denoted
\mathsf{LS}(\varphi)(M)\in\mathsf{LS}(Y)
, to be the
\widehat{Y}
-linear system
\mathsf{LS}(\varphi)(M)=(T,N^{\textnormal{in}},N^{\textnormal{mid}},N^{\textnormal{out}})
, where
T\coloneqq S
and

N^{\textnormal{in}}\coloneqq M^{\textnormal{in}}\Phi^{\textnormal{in}},\qquad N^{\textnormal{mid}}\coloneqq M^{\textnormal{mid}}+M^{\textnormal{in}}\Phi^{\textnormal{mid}}M^{\textnormal{out}},\qquad N^{\textnormal{out}}\coloneqq\Phi^{\textnormal{out}}M^{\textnormal{out}}.

(21)

Theorem 4.18
.

The assignments
X\mapsto\mathsf{LS}(X)
and
\varphi\mapsto\mathsf{LS}(\varphi)
, with parallel composition as in Definition
4.8
constutute a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{LS}\colon{W}_{\mathbf{Lin}}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Proof.

For any pair
X\xrightarrow{\varphi}Y\xrightarrow{\psi}Z
of composable wiring diagrams and linear system
M\in\mathsf{LS}(X)
, one must check that
\mathsf{LS}(\psi\circ\varphi)(M)=\mathsf{LS}(\psi)\big(\mathsf{LS}(\varphi)(M)\big)
, but this follows easily from Lemma
4.16
and Definition
4.17
. It is lax monoidal because direct sum appropriately commutes with the formulas in (
21
).∎

Continuous systems

Definition 4.19
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to Y
be a wiring diagram in
{W}_{\mathbf{Euc}}
, and suppose that
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{dyn}})\in\mathsf{CS}(X)
is an
\widehat{X}
-continuous system. We define
the
\mathsf{CS}
-application of
\varphi
to
F
, denoted
\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)(F)\in\mathsf{CS}(Y)
, to be the
\widehat{Y}
-continuous system
\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)(F)=(T,g^{\textnormal{rdt}},g^{\textnormal{dyn}})
where

T\coloneqq S,\quad g^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\coloneqq\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}\left(f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\right),\quad g^{\textnormal{dyn}}(y,s)\coloneqq f^{\textnormal{dyn}}\left(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\left(y,f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\right),s\right)

(22)

Theorem 4.20
.

The assignments
X\mapsto\mathsf{CS}(X)
and
\varphi\mapsto\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)
, with parallel composition as in Definition
4.9
constitute a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{CS}\colon{W}_{\mathbf{Euc}}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Proof.

Although this Theroem takes place in a different context than that of Theorem
4.12
, namely that of continuous rather than discrete dynamical systems, the formulas (
18
) and (
22
) are identical, and one can check that a virtually identical proof suffices here.∎

Matrices

Definition 4.21
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to Y
be a wiring diagram in
{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}
, and suppose that
M\in\mathsf{Mat}(X)
is a
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-matrix. We define
the
\mathsf{Mat}
-application of
\varphi
to
M
, denoted
N=\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi)(M)\in\mathsf{Mat}(Y)
, to be the
(\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}})
-matrix with
(i,j)
-entry

N_{i,j}\coloneqq\sum_{k\in(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}})^{-1}(j)}M_{\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}(i,k),k}

(23)

for any
i\in\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
j\in\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}}
.

Example 4.22
.

We want to show that the formula in Definition
4.21
reduces to the usual matrix multiplication formula in the case of serial composition. We begin by converting our serial composition diagram into a single-inner-box wiring diagram by parallelizing, as discussed in the beginning of Section
15
.

X_{1}
X_{2}
Y

\scriptstyle I
\scriptstyle J
\scriptstyle K

\;X_{1}\boxplus X_{2}\;
Y

\scriptstyle I
\scriptstyle J
\scriptstyle K

Thus we let
X=X_{1}\boxplus X_{2}
, let
M^{1}\in\mathsf{Mat}(X_{1})
and
M^{2}\in\mathsf{Mat}(X_{2})
, and define
M=M^{1}\otimes M^{2}\in\mathsf{Mat}(X)
to be the Kronecker product; see Definition
4.10
. Note that
\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}=I
,
\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}}=K
,
\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}=I\times J
, and
\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}=J\times K
. Then the wiring diagram
\varphi\colon X\to Y
above acts as follows (see (
13
)) on entries:

1.

for
i\in\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
(j_{1},j_{2})\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}
, we have
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}(i,j_{1},j_{2})=(i,j_{1})
,

2.

for
(j_{1},j_{2})\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}
, we have
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}(j_{1},j_{2})=j_{2}.

Define
N=\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi)(M)
. To show that
N=M^{1}M^{2}
, we compute its entries using Equations (
17
) and (
23
):

\displaystyle N_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{50.58878pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle k\in(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}})^{-1}(j)$\@add@centering}}M_{\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}(i,k),k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{50.58878pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle\{(j_{1},j_{2})\mid j_{2}=j\}$\@add@centering}}(M^{1}\otimes M^{2})_{(i,j_{1}),(j_{1},j_{2})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{50.58878pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle j_{1}$\@add@centering}}M^{1}_{i,j_{1}}\cdot M^{2}_{j_{1},j}

\displaystyle=\parbox{50.58878pt}{\centering$M^{1}M^{2}$\@add@centering}

Thus we have shown that, armed with the Kronecker product formula for parallel composition (Definition
4.10
) and the formula for arbitrary wiring diagrams (Definition
4.21
), we reproduce the the matrix multiplication formula for serial wiring diagrams, as in Example
2.24
.

Example 4.23
.

We want to show that the formula in Definition
4.21
reduces to the usual partial trace formula in the case of feedback composition. Consider the following wiring diagram
\varphi\colon X\to Y
:

X
Y

\scriptstyle I
\scriptstyle J
\scriptstyle K
\scriptstyle K

Analogously to Example
4.22
, we find that
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}(k,j,i)=(k,i)
and
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}(k,j)=j
. Define
N=\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi)(M)
. To show that
N=\Tr^{K}_{I,J}(M)
is the partial trace, as defined in (
8
), we compute its entries using Equation (
23
):

\displaystyle N_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{65.04256pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle(k,j)\in(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}})^{-1}(j)$\@add@centering}}M_{\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}(k,j,i),(k,j)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{65.04256pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle k\in K$\@add@centering}}M_{(k,i),(k,j)}=\Tr^{K}_{I,J}(M).

One can repeat Examples
4.22
and
4.23
for splitting wires as in Example
2.26
; we leave this to the reader. We now prove (in Theorem
4.24
) that one can make arbitrarily complex wiring diagrams and the matrix formula given in (
23
) is consistent with regard to nesting. This theorem holds for matrices over any semiring
R
, so we elide the subscript.

Theorem 4.24
.

The assignments
X\mapsto\mathsf{Mat}(X)
and
\varphi\mapsto\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi)
, with parallel composition as in Definition
4.10
constitute a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathsf{Mat}\colon{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Proof.

We need to check that for any
X\xrightarrow{\varphi}Y\xrightarrow{\psi}Z
and matrix
M\in\mathsf{Mat}(X)
, the following equation holds:

\mathsf{Mat}(\psi)\big(\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi)(M)\big)=\mathsf{Mat}(\psi\circ\varphi)(M).

We again simply compute the
(i,j)
-entries, using Equation (
23
), and show they agree:

\displaystyle\mathsf{Mat}(\psi)\big(\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi)(M)\big)_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{65.04256pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle\ell\in(\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{out}}})^{-1}(j)$\@add@centering}}\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi)(M)_{\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{in}}}(i,\ell),\ell}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{65.04256pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle\ell\in(\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{out}}})^{-1}(j)$\@add@centering}}\left(\;\sum_{k\in(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}})^{-1}(\ell)}M_{\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\left(\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{in}}}(i,\ell),k\right),k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{65.04256pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle k\in\left(\widehat{(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{out}}}\right)^{-1}(j)$\@add@centering}}M_{\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\left(\widehat{\psi^{\textnormal{in}}}\left(i,\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}(k)\right),k\right),k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{65.04256pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle k\in\left(\widehat{(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{out}}}\right)^{-1}(j)$\@add@centering}}M_{\widehat{(\psi\circ\varphi)^{\textnormal{in}}}(k),k}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Mat}(\psi\circ\varphi)(M)_{i,j}

where the penultimate equation follows from Lemma
3.12
.

Checking that it is monoidal involves a similar computation. Let
\varphi_{1}\colon X_{1}\to X_{1}^{\prime}
and
\varphi_{2}\colon X_{2}\to X_{2}^{\prime}
, let
M^{1}\in\mathsf{Mat}(X_{1})
and
M^{2}\in\mathsf{Mat}(X_{2})
. Then for
(i_{1},i_{2})\in\widehat{X_{1}^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X_{2}^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
(j_{1},j_{2})\in\widehat{X_{1}^{\textnormal{out}}}\times\widehat{X_{2}^{\textnormal{out}}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi_{1}\boxplus\varphi_{2})(M^{1}\otimes M^{2})_{(i_{1},i_{2}),(j_{1},j_{2})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\parbox{86.72377pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle k\in\left(\widehat{(\varphi_{1}\boxplus\varphi_{2})^{\textnormal{out}}}\right)^{-1}(j_{1},j_{2})$\@add@centering}}(M^{1}\otimes M^{2})_{\widehat{(\varphi_{1}\boxplus\varphi_{2})^{\textnormal{out}}}((i_{1},i_{2}),k),k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\parbox{86.72377pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle k_{1}\in\left(\widehat{\varphi_{1}^{\textnormal{out}}}\right)^{-1}(j_{1})$\@add@centering}\\
\parbox{86.72377pt}{\centering$\scriptstyle k_{2}\in\left(\widehat{\varphi_{2}^{\textnormal{out}}}\right)^{-1}(j_{2})$\@add@centering}\end{subarray}}M^{1}_{\widehat{\varphi_{1}^{\textnormal{out}}}(i_{1},k_{1}),k_{1}}\cdot M^{2}_{\widehat{\varphi_{2}^{\textnormal{out}}}(i_{2},k_{2}),k_{2}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi_{1})(M^{1})\otimes\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi_{2})(M^{2})

∎

16
Compositional mappings between open systems and matrices

In this section we define a few maps between various interpretations of the wiring diagram syntax. Each of these will be compositional, meaning that one can interconnect a system of systems and then apply the map, or apply the maps and then interconnect, and the result will be the same.

First we show that Euler’s method of approximating an ordinary differential equation by
\epsilon
-steps is compositional, whether one targets discrete systems or measurable systems. Second we show that the steady state matrix—starting from discrete systems, measurable systems, or continuous systems—is also compositional. At this point, we will have achieved our goal of compositionally classifying any of these sorts of open dynamical systems using steady state matrices. Third we show that while the entries of ordinary matrices are used to count the number of steady states, we can actually "remember them" by having the entries be the sets themselves. Finally, we discuss linear stability for continuous dynamical systems and show that it too is compositional, thus recovering a fully compositional generalization of bifurcation diagrams.

Euler’s
\epsilon
-approximation is compositional

Note that any Euclidean space
S
has an underlying vector space (which we denote the same way). For any point
s\in S
there is a canonical linear isomorphism
TS_{s}\xrightarrow{\cong}S
. Thus for any real number
\epsilon
and element
v\in TS_{s}
, the formula
s+\epsilon\cdot v
makes sense, where
\cdot
represents scalar multiplication. The following definition formalizes Construction
2.19
.

Definition 4.25
.

Let
X\in{W}
be a box, and let
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{dyn}})\in\mathsf{CS}(X)
be a continuous dynamical system. Its
\epsilon
-approximation is the discrete dynamical system
\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}(F)=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}}_{\epsilon})\in\mathsf{DS}(X)
, with the same state set and readout function, but where for any
x\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
s\in S
, we define

f^{\textnormal{upd}}_{\epsilon}(x,s)\coloneqq s+\epsilon\cdot f^{\textnormal{upd}}(x,s).

Above we have elided forgetful functors, namely the underlying vector space and underlying set functors,
\mathbf{Euc}\to\mathbf{Vect}
and
\mathbf{Euc}\to\mathbf{Set}
. The following theorem says that if one discretizes each continuous dynamical system in a coupled network and puts them together, the result will be the same as putting the continuous systems together and then discretizing.

Theorem 4.26
.

For any
\epsilon>0
, the
\epsilon
-approximation function
\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}\colon\mathsf{CS}\to\mathsf{DS}
is compositional, i.e., a monoidal natural transformation of
{W}
-algebras.

Proof.

We want to show that
\epsilon
-approximation is a monoidal natural transformation,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W}_{\mathbf{Euc}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Set}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W}_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CS}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overset{\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}}{\Rightarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}}

where
{W}_{U}
is the forgetful functor that comes from the product-preserving functor
U\colon\mathbf{Euc}\to\mathbf{Set}
sending a Euclidean space to its underlying set of points. In the discussion below, we drop subscripts for ease of exposition.

First we must check that for every wiring diagram
\varphi\colon X\to Y
in
{W}
, the diagram below commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CS}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CS}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)}

which establishes that
\epsilon
-approximation is a natural transformation. This is a matter of combining Definitions
4.11
and
4.19
with Definition
4.25
: for any
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{CS}(X)
, both sides give

\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)\big(\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}(F)\big)=s+\epsilon\cdot f^{\textnormal{upd}}\Big(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\big(y,f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\big),s\Big)=\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}\big(\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)(F)\big).

Second we check that
\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}
is monoidal, i.e., that for any boxes
X_{1},X_{2}\in{W}
, the diagram below commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CS}(X_{1})\boxtimes\mathsf{CS}(X_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CS}(X_{1}\boxplus X_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}(X_{1})\boxtimes\mathsf{DS}(X_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}(X_{1}\boxplus X_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxtimes}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}\times\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boxtimes}

By Definitions
4.6
and
4.9
, this comes down to checking that for
f_{1}\in\mathsf{CS}(X_{1})
and
f_{2}\in\mathsf{CS}(X_{2})
, we have

\left(f_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}}\right)_{\epsilon}\times\left(f_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}}\right)_{\epsilon}=\left(f_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}}\times f_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}}\right)_{\epsilon}.

This in turn follows from the fact that
\epsilon
-approximation (Definition
4.25
) preserves products, i.e., for
(a_{1},s_{1})\in\mathsf{CS}(X_{i})
we have

\left(s_{1}+\epsilon\cdot f_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}}(a_{1},s_{1}),s_{2}+\epsilon\cdot f_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}}(a_{2},s_{2})\right)=(s,t)+\epsilon\cdot\left(f_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}}(a_{1},s_{1}),f_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}}(a_{2},s_{2})\right)

completing the proof.
∎

Remark 4.27
.

We could also consider the
\epsilon
-approximation function as a map
\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}\colon\mathsf{CS}\to\mathsf{MS}
. First we need a monoidal functor
{W}_{U}\colon{W}_{\mathbf{Euc}}\to{W}_{\mathsf{MS}}
; this is given by the product-preserving functor
U\colon\mathbf{Euc}\to\mathsf{MS}
sending a Euclidean space to its underlying countably-separated measurable space of Borel sets (see Proposition
2.12
). The only other difference with Definition
4.26
is that we must specify a measure on the underlying measurable space
U(S)
. We use the canonical measure, given by integrating the volume form, that exists on any Euclidean space, or more generally, on any oriented manifold.

Steady state matrices

In Definition
2.4
we introduced the notion of steady states for discrete dynamical systems. In Definition
4.28
we gather these into a matrix, and in Theorem
4.29
we show that this mapping is compositional. That means that if we only know the steady states of each individual dynamical system in a coupled network, we can determine the steady states of their interconnection.
4
4

4

In fact, Theorem
4.29
is only about measuring the sets of steady states. If one wants access to the actual states themselves, see Theorem
4.38
.

Definition 4.28
.

Let
X\in{W}
be a box, and let
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{DS}(X)
be a discrete dynamical system. Its
matrix of steady states
is the
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-matrix,
\textnormal{Stst}(F)\in\mathsf{Mat}(X)
given in Definition
2.28
. That is, its
(i,j)
-entry is defined by the number of steady states

M_{i,j}=\#\left\{s\in S\mid f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)=j,\quad f^{\textnormal{upd}}(i,s)=s\right\}

(24)

for
i\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}},j\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}
.

Theorem 4.29
.

The steady state map
\textnormal{Stst}\colon\mathsf{DS}\to\mathsf{Mat}
is compositional, i.e., a monoidal natural transformation of
{W}
-algebras.

Proof.

First we must check that for every wiring diagram
\varphi\colon X\to Y
in
{W}
, the diagram below commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)}
Stst
Stst
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi)}

We compute both sides, using Equations (
18
), (
24
), and (
23
), on an arbitrary
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{DS}(X)
:

\displaystyle\textnormal{Stst}\big(\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)(F)\big)_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\#\left\{s\in S\mid\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}\big(f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\big)=j,\textnormal{ and }f^{\textnormal{upd}}\Big(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\big(i,f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)\big),s\Big)=s\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}})^{-1}(j)}\#\left\{s\in S\mid f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)=k,\textnormal{ and }f^{\textnormal{upd}}\big(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}(i,k),s\big)=s\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Mat}(\varphi)\big(\textnormal{Stst}(F)\big)_{i,j}.

The middle equality follows because the sets
\left\{s\in S\mid f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)=k,\textnormal{ and }f^{\textnormal{upd}}\big(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}(i,k),s\big)=s\right\}
are disjoint for varying values of
k
. It is easy to show that the functor
Stst
is monoidal; in particular,

\displaystyle\textnormal{Stst}(F_{1}\boxtimes F_{2})_{(i_{1},i_{2}),(j_{1},j_{2})}

\displaystyle=\#\left\{(s_{1},s_{2})\in S_{1}\times S_{2}\;\;\middle|\;\;\parbox{173.44756pt}{$(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s_{1},s_{2})=(j_{1},j_{2}),\\
(f_{1}\boxtimes f_{2})^{\textnormal{upd}}\big((i_{1},i_{2}),(s_{1},s_{2})\big)=(s_{1},s_{2})$}\right\}

\displaystyle=\#\left\{(s_{1},s_{2})\in S_{1}\times S_{2}\;\;\middle|\;\;\parbox{144.54pt}{$f_{1}^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s_{1})=j_{1},f_{1}^{\textnormal{upd}}(i_{1},s_{1})=s_{1},\\
f_{2}^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s_{2})=j_{2},f_{2}^{\textnormal{upd}}(i_{2},s_{2})=s_{2}$}\right\}

\displaystyle=\big(\textnormal{Stst}(F_{1})\otimes\textnormal{Stst}(F_{2})\big)_{(i_{1},i_{2}),(j_{1},j_{2})}

∎

Lemma 4.30
.

Suppose that
f\colon A\times B\to B
and
g\colon B\to C
are measurable functions between countably-separated measurable spaces. Then for any
a\in A
and
c\in C
, the set

X=\{b\in B\mid g(b)=c,\quad f(a,b)=b\}

is measurable.

Proof.

By Proposition
2.12
, the singleton
\{c\}\subseteq C
is measurable, so the set
X_{1}\coloneqq g^{-1}(c)\subseteq B
is measurable. Consider now the composite function

B\xrightarrow{\cong}\{a\}\times B\to A\times B\xrightarrow{f}B.

It is the composition of measurable functions, so again by Proposition
2.12
its fixed point set
X_{2}=\{b\mid f(a,b)=b\}
is measurable. Then
X=X_{1}\cap X_{2}
is the intersection of measurable sets.
∎

Definition 4.31
.

Let
X\in{W}
be a box, and let
F=(S,\mu,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{MS}(X)
be a measurable system. For any
i\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
j\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}
, the set

\widetilde{M}_{i,j}=\left\{s\in S\mid f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)=j,\quad f^{\textnormal{upd}}(i,s)=s\right\}

(25)

is measurable by Lemma
4.30
. Thus we can define the
matrix of steady states
of
F
to be the
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-matrix
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(F)=M
with
(i,j)
-entry defined by the measure

M_{i,j}=\mu\left(\widetilde{M}_{i,j}\right).

Corollary 4.32
.

The steady state mapping
\textnormal{Stst}\colon\mathsf{MS}\to\mathsf{Mat}
is compositional, i.e., a monoidal natural transformation of
{W}
-algebras.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to that of Theorem
4.29
, with the measure
\mu
substituted for the count
\#
.
∎

The same idea works for continuous dynamical systems.

Corollary 4.33
.

The steady state mapping for continuous dynamical systems, as in Definition
2.20
, is compositional, and for any
\epsilon>0
the following diagram of
{W}
-algebras commutes and is natural in
X
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{CS}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Stst}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Stst}(X)}

Proof.

If the diagram commutes for any
X
, then clearly
\textnormal{Stst}\colon\mathsf{CS}\to\mathsf{Mat}
is compositional by Theorems
4.26
and
4.29
. To see that it commutes for any
X
, we simply appeal to Definitions
4.28
,
2.20
, and
4.25
. That is,
s\in S
is a steady state for the
\epsilon
-approximation
\textnormal{Appx}_{\epsilon}(f)
of
f
at input
x
when

s=f^{\textnormal{upd}}_{\epsilon}(x,s)=s+\epsilon\cdot f^{\textnormal{dyn}}(x,s)

Since
\epsilon>0
, this equation holds if and only if
f^{\textnormal{dyn}}(x,s)=0
, i.e., when
s
is a steady state of
f
.
∎

Remembering, rather than measuring, the steady states

Above, we counted the number of steady states, but it is often useful to keep track of the steady states themselves. For this we provide another algebra on
{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}
—i.e., a sixth "intrepretation"—whose formulas will look very much like those of matrices.

Definition 4.34
.

Let
A,B\in\mathbf{Set}
be sets. We define an
(A,B)
-matrix of sets
to be a function
M\colon A\times B\to\mathbf{Set}
; it assigns to each pair
(a,b)
a set
M_{a,b}
.

If
X=(X^{\textnormal{in}},X^{\textnormal{out}})\in{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}
is a
\mathbf{Set}
-box, define
\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X)
to be the set of
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}},\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-matrices of sets:

\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X)\coloneqq\left\{M\colon\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\to\mathbf{Set}\right\}.

Remark 4.35
.

Compare Definition
4.34
with Definition
4.5
for ordinary matrices; the difference is that here is that
\mathbf{Set}
is a category-theoretic version of semiring, with addition given by disjoint union (denoted
\sqcup
) and mutliplication given by cartesian product. Similarly, it is useful to compare Definition
4.36
with Definition
4.10
for parallel composition, and Definition
4.37
with Definition
4.21
for wiring.

Definition 4.36
.

Suppose we are given
M^{1}\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X_{1})
and
M^{2}\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X_{2})
. Their
parallel composition
, denoted
M^{1}\times M^{2}\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X_{1}\boxplus X_{2})
, is defined by

(M^{1}\times M_{2})_{(i_{1},j_{1}),(i_{2},j_{2})}\coloneqq M^{1}_{i_{1},j_{1}}\times M^{2}_{i_{2},j_{2}}

for any
(i_{1},i_{2})\in\widehat{X_{1}^{\textnormal{in}}}\otimes\widehat{X_{2}^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
(j_{1},j_{2})\in\widehat{X_{2}^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X_{2}^{\textnormal{out}}}
.

Definition 4.37
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to Y
be a wiring diagram in
\mathbf{Set}
, and suppose that
M\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X)
is a
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-matrix of sets. We define
the
\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}
-application of
\varphi
to
M
, denoted
N=\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(\varphi)(M)\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(Y)
to be the
(\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}})
-matrix of sets with entries

N_{i,j}\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{k\in(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}})^{-1}(j)}M_{\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}(i,k),k}

(26)

for any
i\in\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
j\in\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}}
.

Theorem 4.38
.

The assignments
X\mapsto\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X)
and
\varphi\mapsto\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(\varphi)
, with parallel composition as in Definition
4.36
constitute a symmetric monoidal functor
\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}\colon{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Proof.

If
X\xrightarrow{\varphi}Y\xrightarrow{\psi}Z
are maps and
(S,M)\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X)
is a matrix of sets, the proof that
\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(\psi)\big(\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(\varphi)(M)\big)=\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(\psi\circ\varphi)(M)
is similar to that of Theorem
4.24
. The only difference is that
\sum
is replaced by
\bigsqcup
; compare (
23
) and (
26
).
∎

Definition 4.39
.

Let
X\in{W}
be a box, and let
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{upd}})\in\mathsf{DS}(X)
be a discrete dynamical system. Its
matrix of steady state-sets
is the
(\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}})
-matrix of sets, denoted
M=\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(F)\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X)
, with
(i,j)
-entry defined by the set steady states

M_{i,j}\coloneqq\left\{s\in S\mid f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s)=j,\quad f^{\textnormal{upd}}(i,s)=s\right\}

(27)

for
i\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}},j\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}
.
The above can be repeated with
\mathsf{CS}
in place of
\mathsf{DS}
, using Definition
2.20
.

Theorem 4.40
.

The steady state-set mapping
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}\colon\mathsf{DS}\to\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}
is compositional, i.e., a monoidal natural transformation of
{W}
-algebras, as is the count function
\#\colon\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}\to\mathsf{Mat}
, and the following diagram of
{W}_{\mathbf{Set}}
-algebras commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{DS}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mat}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}}
Stst
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\#}

The same holds with
\mathsf{DS}
replaced by
\mathsf{CS}
.

Proof.

Recalling Equations (
24
) and (
27
), it is clear that for any
X\in{W}
, the above diagram commutes at
X
. It remains to show that
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}
and
\#
are monoidal natural transformations. The proof that
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}
is monoidal is almost identical to the proof of Theorem
4.29
(which says
Stst
is monoidal), except with
\sum
replaced by
\bigsqcup
. It is easy to show that
\#\colon\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}\to\mathsf{Mat}
is a monoidal transformation; for example one invokes the fact that count preserves products,
\#A\cdot\#B=\#(A\times B)
.

The results for
\mathsf{MS}
and
\mathsf{CS}
follow similarly, from Corollary
4.33
.
∎

Example 4.41
.

We repeat Example
2.29
, except with state set matrices rather than merely their counts. Recall that two dynamical systems are put into series, the first of which comes from Example
2.5
. The state-set matrix for
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, as well as their product, are below

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}\{2\}&\emptyset&\emptyset\\
\emptyset&\{1,4\}&\emptyset\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\{p\}&\emptyset\\
\{p,r\}&\emptyset\\
\emptyset&\{q\}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\{(2,p)\}&\emptyset\\
\{(1,p),(1,r),(4,p),(4,r)\}&\emptyset\end{array}\right)

Linear stability

As discussed in Remark
2.21
, what we have been calling "steady state matrices" are related to bifurcation diagrams; they are more expressive in some respects and less expressive in others. They are more expressive in that we allow arbitrary readout functions, and consider the number of fixed points for each parameter and readout value; this is a very mild generalization. Conversely, steady state matrices are much less expressive than birufcation diagrams in that they do not express stability properties. In this section, we briefly discuss how to classify the linear stability of the steady states for an interconnected system
Y
of dynamical systems, in terms of the stability properties of the individual components
X_{1},\ldots,X_{p}
.
5
5

5

We do not discuss other sorts of stability (e.g., Lyopunov) or any more detailed classification of fixed points here, prefering to leave those for a more advanced study.

The problem is that doing so is impossible if one’s understanding of "classifying linear stability" is too narrow. For open continuous dynamical systems as shown here:

\begin{aligned} \dot{x}_{1}&=f_{1}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\\
\dot{x}_{2}&=f_{2}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\\
&\vdots\\
\dot{x}_{n}&=f_{n}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{k},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\end{aligned}\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} b_{1}&=g_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\\
b_{2}&=g_{2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\\
&\vdots\\
b_{\ell}&=f_{\ell}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\end{aligned}

it is not enough to know the Jacobian of
f
, where the parameters
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
are held constant. Instead, one must remember three Jacobians:
\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial a}\right)
,
\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}\right)
, and
\left(\frac{\partial g}{\partial x}\right)
for each subsystem. By
generalized bifurcation diagram
we mean the steady state matrix, together with these three Jacobians at each steady state. We will show that given the generalized bifurcation diagram for each dynamical system in a coupled network, we can recover the generalized bifurcation diagram for the interconnected system. To make this precise, we will define one more algebra on
{W}
, but first a brief example.

Example 4.42
.

We will put a dynamical system into
X
, and interconnect it as shown right, to form a dynamical system in box
Y
.

X
\scriptstyle A
\scriptstyle B_{1}
\scriptstyle B_{2}

X
Y

\scriptstyle A
\scriptstyle B
\scriptstyle B

(28)

We suppose a 1-dimensional state space,
S=\mathbb{R}
, with dynamics
\dot{x}=2x-3b_{1}+a
and readout map
b_{2}=x
. Note that for any constant input parameter
(a,b)
, the Jacobian is the
1\times 1
-matrix
(2)
, which has a positive real eigenvalue of 2, so the equilibrium at
x=\frac{3b_{1}-a}{2}
is unstable. By Definition
4.19
, the interconnected system formed in
Y
will have dynamics
\dot{x}=-x+a
and readout map
b=x
. This system is stable for any constant input, because the Jacobian is
(-1)
.

Thus, when systems are going to be interconnected, it is not enough to remember the Jacobian of the individual systems as though their parameters were constant. Indeed, when one system’s output is fed into another system as input, the parameters in the second are dependent on the readout—and thus on the state evolution—of the first. It follows that we need to keep around how the readout changes with respect to the state, and how the state changes with respect to the input.

The way one typically approaches a dynamical system is to find the steady states (equilibria) and linearize around them. We have developed a matrix-arithmetic approach that tells us the steady states of an interconnected system
Y
of dynamical systems
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
, given the steady states of each
X_{i}
; this is the content of Theorem
4.29
. Now we want to understand the linear characteristics of each steady state in
Y
given the linear characteristics of their contributors in the
X_{i}
; however, as we showed in Example
4.42
, we need to keep around extra data. Our goal has become to explain what extra data must be assigned to each steady state at the local level in order to produce the same data again at the global level.

We begin with an abstract construction, which says that for any interpretation
Q
of wiring diagrams, one can form matrices of steady states, each of which is assigned an element of
Q
. The only time we will actually use this is when
Q=\mathsf{LS}
is the linear system interpretation.

Recall that for any set
P
, an object of
\mathbf{Set}_{/P}
—called the
slice category of
\mathbf{Set}
over
P
—is a pair
(X,p)
, where
X
is a set and
p\colon X\to P
is a function.

Definition 4.43
.

Let
Q\colon{W}\to\mathbf{Set}
be a symmetric monoidal functor. For any box
X=(X^{\textnormal{in}},X^{\textnormal{out}})\in{W}
, define an
X
-matrix of
Q(X)
’s
, denoted
\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{Q}(X)
to be a function
A\times B\to\mathbf{Set}_{/Q(X)}
; it assigns to each pair
(a,b)\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}
a set
M_{a,b}
, called the
underlying matrix
, and a function
m_{a,b}\colon M_{a,b}\to Q(X)
, called the
Q
-assignment for
M
.

Given
(M_{1},m_{1})\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{Q}(X_{1})
and
(M_{2},m_{2})\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{Q}(X_{2})
, the underlying matrix for the parallel composition is the parallel composition
(M_{1}\otimes M_{2})\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(X_{1}\boxplus X_{2})
of the underlying matrices (as in Definition
4.36
). The
Q
-assignment is given by composing with the parallel composition in
Q
. That is, for any
(a_{1},b_{1})\in\widehat{X_{1}^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X_{1}^{\textnormal{out}}}
and
(a_{2},b_{2})\in\widehat{X_{2}^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X_{2}^{\textnormal{out}}}
, use the composite

(M_{1}\otimes M_{2})_{(a_{1},b_{1}),(a_{2},b_{2})}=(M_{1})_{a_{1},b_{1}}\times(M_{2})_{a_{2},b_{2}}\xrightarrow{m_{a_{1},b_{1}}\times m_{a_{2},b_{2}}}Q(X_{1})\times Q(X_{2})\to Q(X_{1}\boxplus X_{2})

where the last map is parallel composition in
Q
.

Suppose that
\varphi\colon X\to Y
is a wiring diagram, and that
(M,m)\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{Q}(X)
is an
X
-matrix of
Q(X)
’s. We have defined a matrix
N\coloneqq\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(\varphi)(M)
in Definition
4.37
. By the formula (
26
) shown there, for any
(i,j)\in\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}}
, the set
N_{i,j}
is the coproduct of various sets of the form
M_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
, each of which comes with a function
m_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\colon M_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\to Q(X)
. By the universal property of coproducts, these maps induce a unique map
N_{i,j}\to Q(X)
, and we let
n_{i,j}
be the composite
N_{i,j}\to Q(X)\xrightarrow{Q(\varphi)}Q(Y)
.

Given our work above, e.g., the proof of Theorem
4.38
, the following theorem becomes straightforward.

Theorem 4.44
.

For any symmetric monoidal functor
Q\colon{W}\to\mathbf{Set}
, the construction of
\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{Q}
given in Definition
4.43
constitutes a symmetric monoidal functor
\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{Q}\colon{W}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

Linearizing around steady states is a compositional mapping.

We will now give a compositional mapping
\mathsf{CS}\to\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{\mathsf{LS}}
. That is, for any continuous dynamical system
F\in\mathsf{CS}(X)
, we will assign a matrix of steady state-sets
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(F)
as in Definition
4.39
, and each steady state
s_{0}
will be assigned an open linear dynamical system on
X
, namely the derivative of
F
at
s_{0}
. The fact that this is compositional means that if we keep track of the generalized bifurcation diagram (steady states depending on parameters, and the respective Jacobians) for each continuous dynamical system in a coupled network, we can recover the generalized bifurcation diagram for the interconnected system.

Definition 4.45
.

Let
X\in{W}
be a box, and let
F=(S,f^{\textnormal{rdt}},f^{\textnormal{dyn}})\in\mathsf{CS}(X)
be a continuous system. For any
i\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
j\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}
, let
M=\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(F)
be the matrix of steady state-sets of
F
, as in Definition
4.39
. We define the
linearization assignment
to be the function
D_{i,j}\colon M_{i,j}\to\mathsf{LS}(X)
which assigns to each steady state
s_{0}\in M_{i,j}
the linear system
\big(T(s_{0}),D_{i,j}^{\textnormal{in}}(s_{0}),D_{i,j}^{\textnormal{mid}}(s_{0}),D_{i,j}^{\textnormal{out}}(s_{0})\big)\in\mathsf{LS}(X)
given by taking various derivatives, as follows. Let
T(s_{0})\coloneqq S
and put:

D_{i,j}^{\textnormal{in}}(s_{0})\coloneqq\partial_{X^{\textnormal{in}}}f^{\textnormal{dyn}}(i,s_{0}),\qquad D_{i,j}^{\textnormal{mid}}(s_{0})\coloneqq\partial_{S}f^{\textnormal{dyn}}(i,s_{0}),\qquad D_{i,j}^{\textnormal{out}}(s_{0})\coloneqq\partial_{S}f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s_{0}).

(29)

Note that these are all independent of
j
. We call the pair
(M,D)\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{\mathsf{LS}}(X)
the
steady state linearization
of
F
, denoted
(M,D)=\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tLin}(F)
.

Example 4.46
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to Y
be the wiring diagram shown in (
28
), and let
F\in\mathsf{CS}(X)
be the dynamical system discussed there, with dynamics
f^{\textnormal{dyn}}(a,b_{1},x)=2x-3b_{1}+a
and readout map
f^{\textnormal{rdt}}=x
. The matrix of steady state sets
M
is given by
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(F)(b_{1},a,b_{2})=\{s\in\mathbb{R}\mid 2s-3b_{1}+a=0,s=b_{2}\}
. Taking derivatives as shown in (
29
), the linearization assignment
D
, at each
(b_{1},a,b_{2})
, is:

D^{\textnormal{in}}(s)=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}-3&1\end{array}\right),\qquad D^{\textnormal{mid}}(s)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}2\end{array}\right),\qquad D^{\textnormal{out}}(s)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1\end{array}\right).

The matrices happen to be independent of
(b_{1},a,b_{2})
, so we dropped the subscripts. It is the middle matrix
D^{\textnormal{mid}}=(2)
that we discussed in Example
4.42
, which showed that
F
is unstable at the fixed point.

The steady state matrix
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(G)
of the interconnected system
G\coloneqq\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)(F)
can be computed as
N=\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(\varphi)(F)
, by Theorem
4.38
. Hence, we find that
N_{a,b}=\{s\in\mathbb{R}\mid-s+a=0\}
. According to Definition
4.43
, the steady state linearization of
G
is
(N,E)
, where
E
is the linearization assignment
E_{a,b}\colon N_{a,b}\to\mathsf{LS}(Y)
given by composing
D
with
\mathsf{LS}(\varphi)
, as in Definition
4.17
. To do so, we need to know the derivative of the wiring diagram, as in Definition
4.15
, which is

\Phi^{\textnormal{in}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}0\\
1\end{array}\right),\qquad\Phi^{\textnormal{mid}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1\\
0\end{array}\right),\qquad\Phi^{\textnormal{out}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1\end{array}\right).

Thus we compute the linearization assignment
E
(again dropping subscripts), by applying (
21
):

\displaystyle E^{\textnormal{in}}(s)\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}-3&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{l}0\\
1\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1\end{array}\right)\qquad E^{\textnormal{out}}(s)\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle E^{\textnormal{mid}}(s)\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{l}2\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}-3&1\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1\\
0\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{l}1\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}-1\end{array}\right)

As we discussed in Example
4.42
, the middle matrix
E^{\textnormal{mid}}=(-1)
tells us that
\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)(F)
is stable at its fixed point.

Theorem 4.47
.

Let
\mathsf{LS}\colon{W}\to\mathbf{Set}
be the algebra of linear systems as in Theorem
4.18
, and let
\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{\mathsf{LS}}\colon{W}\to\mathbf{Set}
be the algebra of matrices of linear systems, as in Definition
4.43
. The steady state linearization
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tLin}\colon\mathsf{CS}\to\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}_{\mathsf{LS}}
from Definition
4.45
is compositional, i.e., it is a monoidal natural transformation of
{W}
-algebras.

Proof.

Given a wiring diagram
\varphi\colon X\to Y
and a continuous system
F\in\mathsf{CS}(X)
, we need to show that
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tLin}\big(\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)(F)\big)=\mathsf{LS}(\varphi)\big(\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tLin}(F)\big)
. The underlying matrices of steady state sets are the same by Theorem
4.40
, say
N\colon\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}}\to\mathbf{Set}
, where
N=\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(\varphi)(M)
for
M=\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(F)\colon\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}\times\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\to\mathbf{Set}
.

It remains to check that the linearization assignments are the same. Fix
i\in\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
j\in\widehat{Y^{\textnormal{out}}}
, and suppose that
s_{0}\in N_{i,j}
. Then by (
26
), there is some
i^{\prime}\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{in}}}
and
j^{\prime}\in\widehat{X^{\textnormal{out}}}
such that
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}(j^{\prime})=j
and
\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}(i,j^{\prime})=i
, and
s_{0}\in M_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}
. Then by (
29
), (
20
), and (
21
) the linearization assignment
E
of
\mathsf{LS}(\varphi)\big(\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tLin}(F)\big)
at
s_{0}
are

\displaystyle E^{\textnormal{in}}=\partial_{X^{\textnormal{in}}}f^{\textnormal{dyn}}(i^{\prime},s_{0})\partial_{Y^{\textnormal{in}}}\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}},\qquad E^{\textnormal{out}}=\partial_{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}\partial_{S}f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s_{0}),

\displaystyle E^{\textnormal{mid}}=\partial_{S}f^{\textnormal{dyn}}(i^{\prime},s_{0})+\partial_{X^{\textnormal{in}}}f^{\textnormal{dyn}}(i^{\prime},s_{0})\partial_{X^{\textnormal{out}}}\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\partial_{S}f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s_{0}).

On the other hand, to compute
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tLin}\big(\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)(F)\big)
, one must take derivatives as in (
29
) of the equations for
\mathsf{CS}(\varphi)
found in (
22
):

g^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s_{0})=\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{out}}}\left(f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s_{0})\right),\quad g^{\textnormal{dyn}}(i,s_{0})=f^{\textnormal{dyn}}\left(\widehat{\varphi^{\textnormal{in}}}\left(i,f^{\textnormal{rdt}}(s_{0})\right),s_{0}\right).

By mildly tedious but straightforward chain rule calculations, quite analagous to those in Lemma
4.16
, one finds that these derivatives agree with the three matrices above.

∎

17
Extended example

In this example, we string together eight discrete dynamical systems. Let’s refer to the following wiring diagram as
\varphi\colon W,X,X,X,X,X,X,Y\longrightarrow Z
:

\scriptstyle W
\scriptstyle X
\scriptstyle X
\scriptstyle X
\scriptstyle X
\scriptstyle X
\scriptstyle X
\scriptstyle Y
Z

(30)

Suppose that each interior box is inhabited by a discrete dynamical system. Below, the transition diagrams are shown: the leftmost one,
w\in\mathsf{DS}(W)
, is shown left; the middle six are all the same,
x\in\mathsf{DS}(X)
, and are shown in the middle; and the rightmost one,
y\in\mathsf{DS}(Y)
, is shown right:

State: a
Readout: T
State: b
Readout: F
FF, FT

The composed dynamical system
z\coloneqq\mathsf{DS}(\varphi)(w,x,x,x,x,x,x,y)
has
2\cdot 3^{6}\cdot 4=5832
states. One can imagine it as a stack of eight parallel layers: a
w
, then six
x
’s, then a
y
, each sending information to the next—with feedback at the end—i.e., the readout of one layer sent forward as the state-change command for the next. A composite state is a choice of one state in each layer.

Rather than write out the 5832-state transition diagram of the layered system, suppose we just want to understand its steady states. We begin by writing down the matrix of steady state sets associated to each system, e.g.,
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(w)\in\bm{\mathcal{M}}\mathsf{at}(w)
. They are shown below with row- and column-labels to keep things clear:

\begin{array}[]{c| ll}\lx@intercol\hfil\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(w)=\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\parbox{39.74872pt}{\tiny~\hskip 9.39545ptOutputs:\\
Is fixed by:}&T&F\\
\hline\cr TT&\{a\}&\{b\}\\
TF&\{a\}&\emptyset\\
FT&\emptyset&\{b\}\\
FF&\emptyset&\emptyset\end{array}\qquad\quad\begin{array}[]{c| ll}\lx@intercol\hfil\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(x)=\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\parbox{39.74872pt}{\tiny~\hskip 9.39545ptOutputs:\\
Is fixed by:}&T&F\\
\hline\cr T&\{1\}&\{2\}\\
F&\emptyset&\{3\}\\
&&\\
&&\end{array}\qquad\quad\begin{array}[]{c| llll}\lx@intercol\hfil\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(y)=\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\hfil\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\parbox{39.74872pt}{\tiny~\hskip 9.39545ptOutputs:\\
Is fixed by:}&TT&TF&FT&FF\\
\hline\cr T&\{p\}&\{q\}&\emptyset&\emptyset\\
F&\{p\}&\emptyset&\{r\}&\emptyset\\
&&\\
&&\end{array}

We will abbreviate tuples by removing parentheses and commas, so that the two-element set
\{(a,b,c),(c,e)\}
is written
\{abc,ce\}
.

We can now use the steady-state formulas (
4.36
) and (
26
) to compute the steady state-set matrix
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(z)
. However, using these fully general formulas is not always the most efficient approach. Following Examples
4.22
and
4.23
, we can instead multiply the matrices for the serial composition

\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(w)\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(x)^{6}\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(y),

and then trace the result. The fact that this will work comes down to the functoriality of
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}
(proven as Theorem
4.38
).

The reader should try calculating the matrix multiplication
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(x)\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(x)
. One can then see that the serial composition of the middle
x
’s, namely
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(x)^{6}
, is

\begin{array}[]{c| ll}\parbox{39.74872pt}{\tiny~\hskip 9.39545ptOutputs:\\
Is fixed by:}&T&F\\
\hline\cr T&\{111111\}&\{111112,111123,111233,112333,123333,233333\}\\
F&\emptyset&\{3333333\}\end{array}

We continue in this way, and calculate
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(w)\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(x^{6})\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(y)
:

\begin{array}[t]{c| llll}\lx@intercol\hfil\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(wx^{6}y)=\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\parbox{39.74872pt}{\tiny~\hskip 9.39545ptOutputs:\\
Is fixed by:}&TT&TF&FT&FF\\
\hline\cr\\
TT&\left\{\parbox{101.17755pt}{$a111111p,a111112p,\\
a111123p,a111233p,\\
a112333p,a123333p,\\
a233333p,b333333p$}\right\}&\{a111111q\}&\left\{\parbox{101.17755pt}{$a111112r,a111123r,\\
a111233r,a112333r,\\
a123333r,a233333r,\\
b333333r$}\right\}&\emptyset\\
\\
TF&\left\{\parbox{101.17755pt}{$a111111p,a111112p,\\
a111123p,a111233p,\\
a112333p,a123333p,\\
a233333p$}\right\}&\{a111111q\}&\left\{\parbox{101.17755pt}{$a111112r,a111123r,\\
a111233r,a112333r,\\
a123333r,a233333r$}\right\}&\emptyset\\
\\
FT&\{b333333p\}&\emptyset&\{b333333r\}&\emptyset\\
\\
FF&\emptyset&\emptyset&\emptyset&\emptyset\end{array}

Finally, we take the partial trace of this matrix to obtain the desired result,
\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(z)
:

\begin{array}[t]{c| ll}\lx@intercol\hfil\mathcal{S}\textnormal{tst}(x)=\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\parbox{39.74872pt}{\tiny~\hskip 9.39545ptOutputs:\\
Is fixed by:}&T&F\\
\hline\cr\\
T&\left\{\parbox{101.17755pt}{$a111111p,a111112p,\\
a111123p,a111233p,\\
a112333p,a123333p,\\
a233333p,b333333p,\\
a111111q$}\right\}&\left\{\parbox{101.17755pt}{$a111112r,a111123r,\\
a111233r,a112333r,\\
a123333r,a233333r,\\
b333333r$}\right\}\\
\\
F&\{b333333p\}&\{b333333r\}\end{array}

This matrix tells us the steady states of the composite dynamical system
z
.

Let’s interpret the results by invoking our image of
z
as a layered system of
w
, the six
x
’s, and
y
. If we input ’T’ to the system our matrix tells us that ’a111223p’ is a steady state outputting ’T’ and that ’b333333r’ is a steady state outputting ’F’. These 8-character strings are the composite states; one in each layer.

It is easy to check that when
y
is in state p, the system
z
outputs ’T’ and that when
y
is in state r, the system outputs ’F’.
6
6

6

Looking at the wiring diagram (
30
), the top output of
y
is output by the system and the second is fed back to
w
, so if
y
outputs ’FT’ then
z
outputs ’F’.

Thus it suffices to see that these two states are fixed by an input of T. We leave it to the reader to check that the output of every layer indeed leaves the state of the next layer fixed.
